The only statement about it is the finding aid's "a Civil War journal describing the battle of Sabine Pass, Texas" (01-003-P09); the folder inventory does not list it separately (Folder 7 has "Copy of George's personal notebook, including financial records and personal recordings", 01-006-P05). Which battle it describes, and whether George was an eyewitness, cannot be determined from the papers. What the letters establish is that George was stationed at Sabine Pass in 1864 (04-003, 06-113, 06-110-P02) and that his sisters expected "old Magruder" might order him "away from Sabine" (06-066-P01, undated but after the tithe tax of 1863). The earliest letters to him in the collection date from January 1863 (Folder 6, not supplied). Nothing in the supplied scans places him at the Pass on 8 September 1863; nothing excludes it.

**The land-grant certificate.** Not among the supplied scans; only the finding aid's "a land grant certificate from the governor of Texas" (01-003-P09). Its date, acreage, class and legal description cannot be determined from the papers. The only land certificate in the scans is Robert Crow's Jasper County second-class certificate No 246 of 10 January 1840, sold to Sublett (05-029-P01), which has nothing to do with George. The papers do show George's own land: an 80-acre tract "described in Vol. Z, Page 58 of the Deed Records" (04-046-P03), his undivided 60 acres of the Winniford 80 (06-003-P01) and twenty acres from Mary jointly with Jesse and Warren (06-004-P02), all in the A. J. Porter survey.

**Ministry at Sylvana.** The endorsement is the direct evidence: "Bro. George Douglas, who is a member of the Church of Christ worshiping at Sylvana Ellis County Texas, has for some length of time been laboring in word and doctrine, and wishing him to continue in the work, we take pleasure in stating that he goes with our full permission & hearty approval. May he realize with the Apostle Paul. 'Woe is me if I preach not the Gospel of Christ' - Ever remembering the Solemn Charge to Timothy to preach the Word &c - / Given by Order of the Church / On the 2nd Lords day in / Sept 1888 / Elders / H. Douglas / H G Orr" (03-003-P01, 03-005-P01, 03-005-P02). The memoir fragment adds: "My father continued his labors with the Church at Sylvana until his death Sept 23 1923. Though opposing the Sunday School he did not withdraw from the congregation but endeavored to present the truth as he understood it" and "buried from the Church that had been his life work … after his death the congregation was torn by dissensions and after a few years ceased to consistently meet" (05-062-P01, 05-061-P01, 05-061-P02). The undated Zella letters show him hosting a protracted meeting at Sylvana with a visiting revivalist, Bro. Dunn, and reporting confessions and baptisms (04-027 to 04-037). Sadie's 1893 letter mentions "the announcement, by Uncle Geo. of the [[Elmwood?]] camp-meeting" in a paper (05-040-P01). The 1887 deed locates "a Church lot" on the west line of the Porter survey adjoining the Winniford tract (06-004-P01).

**What the 1888 endorsement endorses him for.** For preaching: it certifies that he has been "laboring in word and doctrine" and sends him out "to continue in the work" with the congregation's "full permission & hearty approval", i.e. a letter of commendation for an evangelist going to preach beyond his home congregation (03-003-P01). No ordination language, salary or destination is given.

**Douglas Cemetery, 1917.** The papers are silent. No 1917 document and no deed of a cemetery is in the supplied scans. The only burial places named are "the Rawlins cemetery" where Olaf was buried (04-037-P01), "one family grave yard" in Alabama (06-041-P01) and the church "that had been his life work" from which George was buried (05-061-P01).

**Death.** "My father continued his labors with the Church at Sylvana until his death Sept 23 1923 … He died in Ga and / was brought back and buried from the Church" (05-062-P01, 05-061-P01); slip "his death Sept. 23, 1923" (05-060-P01); finding aid "one mentions George's death in 1923" (01-006-P01). The letters of 30 August to about 12 September 1923 from "Union City" describe cancer of the cheek treated with radium (04-048 to 04-077); the envelopes are postmarked "UNION CITY … CA." (04-055-P02, 04-064-P02) and once "GA" (04-071-P02); the letter says the writer will not "come back by Nashville … over the Cumberland Mountains" (04-052-P03). The memoir says he died in Georgia. The postmark readings conflict (see E); the memoir's "Ga" and the Nashville route favour Georgia.

### C.4 "L. Douglas" of the sermon notes

The notes are signed "L. Douglas. / Sylvana Feb 16th 1883" (02-004-P03). The slip writer was unsure: "L(?) Douglas" (02-002-P01). The papers never expand the initial. Persons named Douglas with a name beginning L in the supplied scans are **Lizzie** (Elizabeth) Douglas, George's unmarried sister, who lived at Ferris/Sylvana in 1883 (letters addressed to her at Ferris, 06-012-P01; deeds to her, 05-136-P02), and **Laura** (Robinson/Robison), who was married and by 1893 living in Ventura County, California (05-137-P03). A slip on a Lizzie Graves letter reads "Regards given to Dr. (L. Douglas) & Ellen" (06-052-P01), where the letter itself says "the Dr. and Ellen" (06-054-P01), i.e. Henry the physician and his wife Ellen; that slip-writer's "L." for Henry is unexplained and is the only place the papers attach "L." to a Douglas man of that generation. No "Lynn" other than N. L. (dead in 1874) occurs. No son of George named L. is mentioned. Whether L. Douglas was a brother, a son or Lizzie cannot be settled from the papers; the sermon's citation of Renan and its polished style are consistent with the literary household shown by the poems and compositions in the same folder (05-081, 05-084).

### C.5 Warren Douglas

Every mention, in date order:

1. April 1853: "Jepie's & Warrens absences, he, thinking it probable that they would not be at home until about that time" (05-006-P01).
2. December 1853: "Jepse & Warren had gone out on / Brazos … Warren has been writing a little about his trip to the Brazos, but only casually alluded to his second trip" (04-093-P01, 04-094-P01).
3. January 1855: "I wrote to Warren, of a Cancer case we have on hand" (05-004-P01).
4. June 1860: "write to Warren to do the same as I am very anxious to hear from all of you" (05-011-P01).
5. 12 January 1862: "Beck has not been very well, she stayed all week with me went home on friday. Warren had gone to deer creek to see about moveing her there as he is going to the war as soon as she is confined they will not break up housekeeping until after that, she is expecting about the 5 to the 11 of Feb. … Warren tryed to get some of her sisters to come home with him and stay a while with her" (04-084-P01); "he will leave as soon as beck is able for him to move her to her fathers" (04-085-P01).
6. 15 January 1862: "Warren will join the Army soon. I do not know when I can come down. We are looking for some of you up before Warren starts"; "Warren is likely to get off in Feb — does not know what Co he will join yet" (04-086-P01, 04-086-P02).
7. 28 January 1863: a letter "To George and Warren from Lizzie" is listed in Folder 6 (01-006-P04), not supplied.
8. April 1864: "Beck and [[Wash?]] have had to come [[illegible]] from Grape Vine Springs. On account of water being so scarce" (06-086-P02).
9. September 1864: "Daniel H. Willbanks, Warren [[deleted: knows]] [[inserted: knew]] him" (06-109-P03).
10. 13–16 November 1864: "we have not heard from Warren but look for him and Beck down. Griffin or somebody else must have a particular spite at Warren. If he did not get his furlough lengthened, his stay at home will be short" (06-091-P02); "Warren not got down; he can get no conveyance to bring Beck down while he stays; he wrote he would be here on the 10th of Dec. on his way back to Sabine. I suppose he got his furlough lengthened" (06-092-P01).
11. 13 March 1865: "Kind Regards to Warren & John Your Cousin Henry", in a letter to George in camp (04-011-P04).
12. 21 April 1865: "With best love to Warren and yourself I remain Lizzie" (06-072-P04).
13. 19 February 1875: signs the heirs' quitclaim as "Warren Douglas"; acknowledged in Ellis County as "W. Douglas" (04-042-P03, 04-042-P06).
14. 8 October 1886: signs the heirs' deed to Lizzie "Warren Douglas" (05-135-P03); acknowledged it in **Johnson County** on 27 January 1887 before Jno. T. Odom, Clerk of the District Court (05-154-P04).
15. 1 April 1887: George's twenty acres came "by Deed from Mary Douglas, jointly with Jesse and Warren Douglas and by deeds from the said Jesse and Warren Douglas" (06-004-P02).

**Marriage to Rebecca Ann Evans.** The papers never give her surname or a marriage date. "Beck" is Warren's wife by the context of Ellen's letter (04-084-P01: her confinement, "her sisters", "her fathers") and Lizzie's ("look for him and Beck down", 06-091-P02). Neither 1857 nor 1859 nor Johnson County nor Brushy Creek is attached to the marriage in the papers. The child expected in February 1862 is not named. Johnson County does appear as Warren's residence in January 1887 (05-154-P04).

**Confederate service.** The papers show that Warren was going "to the war" in February 1862 with his company still undecided (04-084-P01, 04-086-P02); that in November 1864 he was on furlough, "Griffin" was thought to have "a particular spite at Warren", and he expected to pass through Brushy "on his way back to Sabine" (06-091-P02, 06-092-P01); that in March and April 1865 he was with George at camp (04-011-P04, 06-072-P04). No unit, company or rank is stated for Warren anywhere in the supplied scans; the words "lieutenant" and "21st" do not occur. Inference (outside knowledge, for verification): a "Griffin" over a Sabine Pass garrison in 1864 fits Lt. Col. W. H. Griffin of the 21st Texas Infantry Battalion, which would place Warren in the same command as George; whether he held a commission is not shown by the papers.

**Arizona, 1887–88, and the wagon train.** The papers are silent. The last trace of Warren is the Johnson County acknowledgment of 27 January 1887 (05-154-P04). Arizona is mentioned once, in 1864, of another man's regiment: "the Reg. to which Eppa belongs has been ordered to Arizona. Another wildgoose chase like that of Sibleys Brigade" (06-103-P02). Gila Bend does not occur.

### C.6 Henry, Jesse, Mary, Rebecca Goodloe, Rebecca Douglas Winniford, Emily and Mary Douglas

**Henry Douglas** (signs "Henry Douglas", "H. Douglas", "J. H. Douglas" on the 1875 quitclaim, 04-042-P03, and "H Douglas" as elder in 1888). Son of N. L. Douglas ("To Mr. N. L. Douglas from son, Henry Douglas", 04-078-P01; "Dear Father", 04-079-P01; "Your affectionate Son. Henry Douglas", 04-081-P01). Left home for Palestine, Anderson County, before September 1851; a carpenter there ("continue journeywork or turn out undertaker", 04-080-P01) at $1.50 a day; a member of the Christian Church at Palestine (04-080-P01) who took the co-operation meeting's affairs to heart (04-094-P01); by January 1855 in medical partnership with Dr Kendrick (05-003-P01) and thereafter "the Dr." (06-054-P01), losing patients in 1864 (06-109-P03). Drove 750 sheep from Kentucky towards Texas in June 1860 (05-009 to 05-011), having left Ellen in Lincoln County (05-011-P01). Married to **Ellen**, who wrote to "Dear Sister Lizzie" in January 1862 from "Sams cabin" with children [[Gaskin?]], Angus and Maggie (04-083, 04-084); at Buchanan in January 1862 (04-086-P01); "on Hack" (Hack Creek) in 1864 (06-086-P02, 06-106-P02, 06-109-P03); visiting Brushy in November 1864 and April 1865 (06-091-P02, 06-072-P01). Cousin Henry's separate identity: the letters signed "Henry" from camp in 1864–65 (04-004 to 04-011) call George "Cousin" and give "Hempstead Tex Co G Browns Regt"; Henry the brother was a physician at home with Ellen in those years (06-091-P02), so these are two different Henrys (inference from the texts). By 1892 Henry lived at Red Oak, had twins Clarence and Kate born 8 September 1878, and wrote as "Your brother Henry" (04-088 to 04-091). He was an elder of the Sylvana church in 1888 (03-005-P02) and signed the 1886 deed (05-135-P03, 05-155-P02). Two other Henrys occur: Henry Orr (06-060-P01, 06-097-P02) and Henry Allen (04-090-P01).

**Jesse Douglas** ("Jeppe", "Jepse", "Jepie", "Jess", "Jessie"). Son of N. L. Douglas (signs the quitclaim "Jeppe Douglas", 04-042-P03; "Your Brother As Ever Jesse Douglas" to George, 05-133-P04). With Warren on the Brazos in 1853 (04-093-P01). His wife **Gail** died on Saturday 23 April 1864 in childbirth: "Jeppe is again a widower. Gail died last Saturday (23d). Mother got home yesterday and brought the little baby with her" (06-106-P02); the baby was named Sarah, "Gale named her that" (06-099-P02); a slip expands the mother's name to "Eveline Gale (Manly) Douglas" (06-105-P02). "Again a widower" implies an earlier wife (inference from the word). Hauled wool to San Antonio in November 1864 (06-092-P01); gathered 130 bushels of corn on Hack Creek (06-109-P04). By August 1869 at Cross Creek, Travis County, married to Nancy with a son Eli and a new son Earl Stewart (born 2 August 1869) and a mother-in-law "the Old Stewarts" (05-123 to 05-133); bought 320 acres in Hood County for Sarah (05-132-P01). Acknowledged the 1875 quitclaim in County of [[Rusk?]] (the family edition reads Hood) on 9 March 1875 (04-043-P07). At Alexander, Texas, in June 1884 arranging a school for "Dear Daughter" Sarah (05-127). His widow Nancy and son Earl signed the 1891 deed in Erath County (05-141-P04, 05-142-P02), so Jesse was dead by 4 June 1891 (inference). Sarah married H. (J. H.) Headrick and became **Sadie** of Bardsdale, California, mother of Elmo and Bruce (05-035 to 05-048).

**Mary Douglas** (N. L.'s wife). See C.1. She kept house at Brushy through the war ("Mother", 06-106-P02), gave the 1874 inventory, deeded her children their portions before February 1875 (04-042-P02), deeded 82 acres to Lizzie with a life estate on 25 February 1875 (05-158-P02), was a first cousin of Rebecca T. Alexander through her mother (05-095-P01), lived at Ferris in 1882–83 (05-095-P01) and was dead by 8 October 1886, when her heirs conveyed her real estate to Lizzie "for and in consideration of Services rendered to the Said Mary Douglas during her last years of life by Lizzie Douglas" (05-136-P02). Her heirs in 1886–91 were George, Warren, Laura Robison, the Stanfords, Farrars, Bells, Headricks, Robisons, H Douglas, Nancy and Earl Douglas, the McDaniels, the Duffs, E L Winiford and the Winniford-Roper-McCrary group (05-135-P03, 05-141-P04, 06-010-P02).

**Rebecca Goodloe.** Named only in the finding aid ("Correspondence from Rebecca Goodloe to her daughters", Folder 4, 01-006-P02; subject index 01-005-P01) and on the slip "daughters of Rebecca Goodloe" with the note "Jacob Wright, brother of Rebecca Wright Goodloe" (05-086-P01). Lillard's letters make her, by inference, the Wright sister through whom Mary Douglas and Cynthia Orr were first cousins of Rebecca T. (Wright) Alexander, daughter of "your uncle Jacob Wright" (05-095-P01). No letter of hers is in the supplied scans.

**Rebecca Douglas Winniford.** The papers never state her parentage or death date. She appears as the person for whose heirs an 80-acre survey in the Porter 1280 was made: "an 80 acre survey made to the heirs of Rebecca Winniford" (05-158-P03, 1875); "surveyed for the heirs of Rebeca Winniford" (06-003-P01); and Lucy I Winniford's fifth "accrued to the said Lucy I Winniford by deed from Mary Douglas jointly with us as heirs of Rebecca Winniford" (05-145-P02). Her heirs in 1890 were E L Winniford, Mary H Roper, H. D. Winniford, Alice E McCrary and the late Lucy I Winniford, five shares (05-145-P02). The finding aid lists letters from "Rebecca Winneford to George Bell" of 1863–64 and from "N. B. Winneford to George Douglas (1863)" in Folder 6 (01-006-P04), not supplied. "Mr Winnifords marriage" is mentioned in November 1868 (06-056-P02) and a slip mentions "Norvell Winneford working as teamster" in 1864 (06-113-P01). That Mary Douglas deeded land to Rebecca's heirs alongside her own children is the papers' only indication that Rebecca was Mary's daughter (inference).

**Emily Douglas (d. 1850, aged 13) and Mary Douglas (d. 1861, aged 16).** The papers are silent on both deaths. "Emily Douglas" occurs only in the finding aid's subject list and "Songs by Emily Douglas" in Folder 4 (01-004-P08, 01-006-P02). A younger Mary Douglas is never distinguished from her mother in the supplied scans; Henry's 1865 condolence on "the Death of your Sister" (04-010-P04, March 1865) refers to a sister who died shortly before, which fits neither 1850 nor 1861 (see E). The mid-1860s letters name living sisters Lizzie, Laura and "Sister"/Sue.

**Sue (Susanna) Bell.** "Susanna Bell" and "J. M. Bell" sign the 1875 quitclaim; she is "wife of the said J. M. Bell" (04-042-P03, 04-042-P07). Henry in 1853 mentions "Susannah" coming down with him (05-006-P01). In the war letters she is "Sue" and "Sister", co-writes the August 1864 letter from the Liberty school house and signs "Sue" (06-103-P02), and has children ("Sisters children", 06-064-P01; "send the children to school", 06-091-P02). L. A. Jetton's letter of March 1878 condoles Lizzie: "I loved Cousin Sue on earth I hope to meet her in heaven" (06-018-P01), and its slip reads "(mentions death[?] of Susanna Douglas bell)" (06-016-P01), so she died before 3 March 1878. Emma Bell's composition (05-084) and "Maggy and Jennie Bell" (06-054-P01) belong to this family (inference).

### C.7 The loan and legal papers of 1854

There is no loan of 1854 in the supplied scans. The dated legal papers of the 1849–55 period are: Crow to Sublett deed, 1849 (A-44); the two Navarro tax redemptions of 1851 in which N L Douglas acts "as agent for G A Sublett" and pays $14.48 and $3.08 (05-020-P02, 05-033-P03); the Farrar note to Sublett for $520 of 12 October 1852, endorsed to Mr [[Ledbetter?]] and with $15 received by Douglas for Sublett on 20 April 1853 (05-017, 05-018); the Sublett power of attorney of 28 October 1852 (05-025); and James Bass's letter of 18 April 1854 asking Douglas to see to the 3,250-acre Littleton White tract and the Louis Dickson tract on the Trinity (05-014). In all of these N. L. Douglas is the trusted agent and collector, not the borrower: Sublett calls him "my friend" (05-025-P01) and Bass "your friend & obt, servt" (05-015-P01). What they reveal about the family's economic position: Douglas had standing and credit in Rutherford County that Tennessee landowners used to manage Texas holdings; he handled their money (the $15 receipt) and their tax redemptions; the family itself, by the 1874 inventory, held 640 acres (94 improved) valued at $4,140 with stock worth about $500 (05-151-P02). The only real "loan papers" are the two Federal Land Bank closing statements of 27 January 1939 for Bruce Headrick with Zella Douglas as co-payee, $1,800 on 70 acres plus a $1,000 second-lien commissioner's loan, total debt capped at $2,800, "To pay balance purchase money lien" (04-040, 04-041); the finding aid describes Folder 1 as containing "loan papers from 1939" (01-005-P04). If the question means the 1854 folder date printed on the Folder 2 and 3 repository covers ("1854", 05-001-P03, 06-001-P03), that is the cover's nominal date, not a loan.

### C.8 The 1939 correspondence and why the collection ends there

The 1939 items are not letters but the two Federal Land Bank of Houston closing statements of 27 January 1939 addressed to the Secretary-Treasurer of the Ellis National Farm Loan Association for the loan of Bruce Headrick of Red Oak, with checks payable jointly to Bruce Headrick and Zella Douglas (04-040, 04-041), and the undated typescript field notes for Bruce Hedrick's 70 acres bounded by the Miss Zella Douglas property and the Geo. Douglas 80-acre tract (04-046). Read together (inference), Zella was selling or financing part of George's old land to Bruce Headrick, her cousin (Sadie's son Bruce, 05-037-P01), and the note dated 11 January 1939 carried a purchase-money lien in her favour. The collection's closing date of 1939 in the repository title reflects these papers; the actual latest item is the New York envelope to C. C. Douglas of 28 February 1941 (04-039-P03). Why the family's papers stop is not stated anywhere in the scans; the finding aid records the source of acquisition as "unknown" (01-003-P11).

### C.9 The Goodloe family of Murfreesboro

- "3 of the Goodloe family" are among the living first cousins of Rebecca T. Alexander (05-095-P01).
- Mary Douglas and Cynthia Orr are "daughters of Rebecca Goodloe" (slip, 05-086-P01) and Mrs Alexander's father Jacob Wright was "your uncle" (05-095-P01).
- Newton Goodloe, "the brother of Mary Douglas & Cynthia Orr", father of Tommie (Thomas), French, Bobbie (Robert), Mary and the writer Fannie (Columbia) (slip, 06-026-P01), was in Texas in June 1874 and "so much pleased with Texas" (06-028-P01); Fannie wrote from Russellville, Alabama, where she kept house for "my Uncle Dr Clarke" and mentions "Uncle John" and a nephew "little N L" (06-027-P01, 06-028-P01).
- M. E. Goodloe wrote from Randolph in February 1865, addressing Lizzie as "Dear Niece" though the slip calls the writer the niece (06-060-P01); she had "a very pretty and a favort cousin" for Lizzie and had corresponded with George (06-061-P02).
- "two Goodloes (Aquilla's sons) have deserted" the Confederate army, and "Squire is safe over the Border" (06-094-P03, February 1864).
- The finding aid indexes L. Columbia Goodloe, M. E. Goodloe and Rebecca Goodloe (01-005-P01) and lists "To Lizzie Douglas from L. Columbia Goodloe (Jan. 21, 1874)" (01-005-P05).
- The Jettons of Readyville call Mary Douglas "Aunt" (05-110-P01, 05-117-P02) and Lizzie "Cousin"; Lewis Jetton calls Cynthia and Mary "My Sisters" (05-111-P01) and the slip "sisters-in law" (05-105-P02). Albert Jetton kept a store in Murfreesboro (05-103-P01). "Old Aunt Polly Wright died about two weeks ago" (05-120-P01, February 1870).

### C.10 Religion and the Stone-Campbell affiliation

The earliest religious statement is Henry's, 8 September 1851, from Palestine: "The Christian Church at this place have resolved to build a house of worship" and "Nothing gratified me more than to hear of Susannahs [[illegible]] the Truth, may she hold out faithful to the end is my sincere prayer. O that all the Family that have arrived at the years of discretion were gathered into the fold of Christ" (04-080-P01, 04-081-P01). In December 1853 he describes the Palestine "Co-operation" that subscribed about $450 "for evangelizing purposes" and appointed managers "to employ an evangelist", with "Bro. Kendrick" preaching and baptisms by immersion of children who "seemed fully to understand … what they were doing" (04-094-P01, 04-095-P01). These are Stone-Campbell ("Christian Church", "co-operation", "evangelist", believer's baptism) in vocabulary, and they show the family already in that fellowship by 1851, with Susannah's conversion news reaching Henry from home. The home congregation was **Liberty** (Liberty Settlement, Red Oak): cousin Henry "attended Liberty 3 Sundays" in 1864 (04-006-P03); Lizzie distinguishes "our meeting" and "the christians" from the Methodists and Presbyterians who also met at Liberty (06-111-P01, 06-112-P01); Lizzie Graves in Alabama misses the "series of doctrinal sermons like they do at Liberty" and laments "I havnt met up with any one yet who is a member of the Christian Church Pa and I are alone" (06-050-P02). Bro Jones "preached one of the best sermons I ever heard on the unity of the church" (06-111-P02) and Parson Austin the Methodist said "they (the christians) have the bulge on us" (06-112-P01). By 1888 the congregation is "the Church of Christ worshiping at Sylvana Ellis County Texas" with elders H. Douglas and H G Orr (03-003-P01, 03-005-P02); in 1892 Henry reports "a good meeting at Liberty conducted by Bros Cole & Graves" and that his children "obeyed the gospel" (04-089-P01). George's later letters use "Bro." for preachers and members, report confessions and baptisms at Sylvana (04-030-P01, 04-035-P03), and refer to "the brethren" and "the A.W." paper (04-077-P01). Sadie in California reads "the Advocate" (05-040-P01) and refuses life insurance as "against my religious convictions" (05-043-P01). The memoir says George opposed the Sunday School but stayed in the congregation (05-062-P01). The papers contain no conversion narrative for N. L. or Mary and no statement of when the family first joined; the affiliation is simply already present in 1851.

---

## D. Quotable passages

Verbatim, with the transcript's markers. Grouped by theme; each passage is cited.

### Leaving home, frontier life, travel

- Henry to his mother, 8 April 1853 (05-007-P01): "I know that while memory lasts I shall never forget the day when I parted from Father, in the wide lonely prairie, near the Willow pond, his parting advice, & the loneliness of the subsequent hours in walking to Porters Bluff. Never till then had I felt so isolated; never had I felt what it was to be homeless, houseless, & in effect, friendless, for I was going amongst strangers, & I knew not whither I took everything upon trust; & have not been disappointed. I have been blest with health & plenty; not so abundantly as some, but as much as I had any right to hope for."
- Henry to his father, 8 September 1851 (04-080-P01): "Were it not so expensive to live here I could make a tolerably fair living. I am now helping [[Hedlie?]] a large Livery Stable, my wages are $1,50 cts pr day, but after subtracting $10 pr month for board & washing, the amount is considerably diminished."
- Henry on the sheep drive, 23 June 1860 (05-011-P01): "Taking everything into consideration I cannot feel otherwise than \"low down\" but win or lose I am bound to go through. My health is still good & if I dont get sick I think I can stand it. Now I have written this I do not know that it would be best to send it, but you had better know the truth than to be in suspense."
- Henry, 23 June 1860 (05-010-P01): "The sheep becomes dull & stupid & in some cases the head swells — under the jaw the skin will hang in a pouch & I have stuck my knife into several & the yellow serum would flow freely."
- Bob to George, 19 September 1863 (04-019-P04): "it has been A long time since we used to meet down on old Brushy and give the old gobblers a round. and I fear it will be a longer one Still before we can have that blessed privilege again. nearly Four years have pass Since I Saw you and I imagined that you are the Same old george you used to be. I have not changed in the least, only from the big to the bigger. I weigh 175 pounds and the largest Devil in co. (F.)"
- Lizzie, 15 April 1864 (06-087-P01): "You ought to be here to hear the turkeys gobble of a morning and see some of our beautiful pet deer. They are very gentle, since you have ceased to torment them. We also have another pet up in these western thickets, which [[illegible]] we suppose to be a panther. There are a kind of pets I would rather would be a little more distant."
- Lizzie, 21 April 1865 (06-072-P01): "They have got the stove set up, but the weather is pretty cool now to be without a fire place. They got the top of the well, a good many old shoes and large rocks and a Young possum out of the well."
- Jesse, 28 November 1869 (05-132-P02): "the above Costing Me My Noble Animal Billy Capshaw — doubt Mcdonalds [[deleted: illegible]] Selim Superior. Well that trade [[necessitated?]] more Sickness throwed [[lost: torn edge]] Me lower down. Now [[lost: torn edge]] is ride a mule, drive a mule [[lost: torn edge]] up hire mule or walk"
- Jesse, 28 November 1869 (05-131-P03): "A moving Stuff goes for nothing But a Buyer has to pay the biggest prices"
- Lizzie Graves, 10 March 1867 (06-038-P01): "it is true I would much rather live in Texas and I sometimes feel right sad when I think about having left there but I am determined to try and be contented wherever my lot may be cast"
- Lizzie Graves, 29 August 1867 (06-050-P01): "\"Cousin Lizzie your sickness shows right smartly on you\" I said \"Oh! it is only helping to make an Ala. girl of me\" it seemed like they were going to [[inserted: quiz]] me they all commenced \"yes you never have any sickness in Texas\" said I \"we dont have as many pale faces there as you do here\""
- George to Zella, 1923 (04-052-P02): "Half moon pies well cooked, sandwiches like you provided for me, make a splendid traveling ration, and a tin cup for water is indispensable."

### The war

- Cousin Henry, 29 January 1864 (04-004-P05): "But Enough on this subject as it is hardly entitled to a place in these times of strife & Confusion - Whilst the all absorbing all engrossing theme is War War - its stern realities & consequences Which at present our Country has begun to feel the Weight of considerably."
- Cousin Henry, 18 September 1863 (04-018-P01): "We had Seen the Elephant in La & the Boys were willing to go any where. They will doubtless See Some other large Animal in Ark for they will have to ride it as they Left it."
- Cousin Henry, 18 September 1863 (04-017-P04): "If Shoutings are an indication of Godliness — This is Certainly the most Christian people in the range of my knowledge. Possibly this may be attributable to the fact of the abundance of Preachers — who Claim exemption by reason of their vocation and fearing if they do not all Preach they will have to Shoulder their musket & Battle Some"
- James at Tupelo, 25 January 1865 (04-021-P03): "I was painfully wounded on the 22nd of July while Charging the enemys works The ball Struck me on the the left wrist and passed throw my hand my wrist Bones was badly fractured also the Bone of the middle finger I have Suffered a great deal with it"
- James, 25 January 1865 (04-023-P04): "Thair is only four of old Co \"I\" left that is able for duty. one to evry 7 is furloughed to go to Texas Brother Lafayette gets a 75 days furlough to go home I am glad that he gets to go and I would like to accompany him very much. But that I Cannot do honorably and I would not go home other wise."
- Lizzie, 19 February 1864 (06-094-P03): "Jim says \"he can stand the fatigue and hardships all very well, till it comes to the fighting fighting, and then when he sees his comrades falling around him, it is all he can do to persuade himself not to run.\" Tole Hamilton fell at Ringold, in the retreat. he was shot through the breast, and only lived a few minutes. Poor fellow he has gone like many another from home and kindred, never more to return. He leaves a widow and child."
- Lizzie, 19 February 1864 (06-094-P03): "But Squire is safe over the Border, where a many another poor Dixie boy wishes he was I expect."
- Lizzie, 19–20 February 1864 (06-095-P02): "But there are men in this confederacy who murder for money and I have some reason to believe, that [[Sherrol?]] is one of them. … He tells of deeds he has committed that would make any honest person shudder — Such as going to a union man's house, passing himself off for a union man get into the others Sentiments, then draw out his pistol and shoot him down. If all his tales are true, we have need to fear him."
- Lizzie, 20 February 1864 (06-095-P03): "he says his command had guns as they were dismounted Cavalry, but some of them were actually armed with sticks for guns."
- Lizzie, 14 April 1864 (06-086-P02): "The story is that Mr White [[illegible]] settled on [[illegible]] to take him, but when they came up to him Mrs White presented a six shooter and told them if they advanced she would shoot them. so they did not take Mr White. Hurrah! for some of the women dont you say so."
- Lizzie, 28 June 1864 (06-097-P02): "I have sorrowful news to tell you this morning. Uncle Alfred is dead. … He died late Sunday evening the 5th of this month, just 18 days after he was wounded. Poor Aunt Bettie is allmost crushed beneath the blow. As soon as she heard it was really true, she fainted away, and they thought for a while she would never revive again."
- Lizzie, 28 June 1864 (06-098-P01): "Poor Uncle Alf. when he told Lou and me goodbye he said \"I just want to get into one big fight, that will do me.\" But now he sleeps between two comrades in arms — John Morgan, and [[deleted: illegible]] [[inserted: Docter]] Gardner. … he watched him during the fight and he appeared to be the coolest man in the Co."
- Lizzie, 28 June 1864 (06-098-P04): "He said he had just recd your letter of 24 pages foolscap giving him an acct of yourself since you entered the service; that you had used up Walker and Websters besides using various / \"Technical terms\""
- Sue, August 1864 (06-102-P01): "We are having rather a wet week this week, [[and?]] (for the consequence) plenty of fleas. They are about as thick as flies down at Sabine, though we have the advantage of a convenient leaf carpet which is a great help."
- Sue, August 1864 (06-103-P02): "His furlough has been out a long time and he says when he is in the army all he thinks about is how to manage to get another. His patriotism has cooled down several degrees below zero."
- Lizzie, 21 August 1864 (06-104-P02): "Tom Witherspoon is now at home with a hole in his head large enough to put your thumb in, the covering of his brain can be seen. Though his mind is uninjured."
- Lizzie, undated (06-066-P01): "What if old Magruder orders you away from Sabine about the time Joda gets back, and takes all of your flour and bacon away from you? We are going to send you a sack of flour a piece and a good supply of fat bacon which I hope will serve to make your fish a little more savory."
- Lizzie, 7 September 1864 (06-110-P03): "Thirty days is a short time for a man to come so far, he would not have long to stay at home but a short time is better than none. I am glad old Magruder is gone."
- Lizzie, 7 September 1864 (06-112-P02): "He says the Soldiers he talked with sayed the would fight us ten years but what they would whip us; that old Abe gave them plenty to eat and wear, and that is all they care for."
- Lizzie, 13 November 1864 (06-090-P01): "If I had any hopes of the war ending soon I would feel so much better but from the tone of the papers, we need not look for peace in four more years. Old Abe is elected in all probability"
- Lizzie, 16 November 1864 (06-092-P01): "They say they had plenty there of every thing and a good house to live in. The jayHawkers were the only ones that pestered them much. They say the feds treated them better than our folks."
- Dock, quoted 13 November 1864 (06-091-P02): "He winds up his letter by saying \"I am a peace man, let it come as it may.\""
- Lizzie, 22 April 1865 (06-070-P02, 06-071-P01): "They hung him on the same limb Stanley was hung on — so we have heard. … One thing is very certain to my mind, that is if they ever captured him, he never made his escape. Perhaps they merited death, but I dread the consequences of this mob law. We know not, but this is the beginning of terrible times. I hate the thought that Brushy [[deleted: should]] should be the place of the beginning of such things. I would feel a great deal better if I knew that man was not hanging over in that thicket."
- Fragment, 1864 (06-118-P01): "to see Jeff Davis! got to drive / his waggons! getting biger pay / than any of us\"!"
- M E Goodloe, 12 February 1865 (06-061-P01): "I did justice to the Supper but of cours tuck no hands in the dance I did not enjoy my self for it remined me of days happy days that have gon nevre to return Lizzie I do not think their is any more purfict happiness in this world for me for my brittest hopes have been blasted"

### Deaths and grief

- Cousin Henry, 13 March 1865 (04-010-P04): "But while enjoying those things one is Drawn in Retrospection and reflecting upon the return of a Long absent Brother all joy has been turned into sadness by the Intelligence of the Death of your Sister. … Cousin George In this dark hour of bereavement you have my Sympathies."
- Lizzie, 30 April 1864 (06-106-P02): "I have sad news to communicate this time; Jeppe is again a widower. Gail died last Saturday (23d). Mother got home yesterday and brought the little baby with her. It is the poorest least one I ever saw, though now it fattens up I think it will be very pretty. Mother says Jeppe told her he would give it to Sue and me, poor little orphan, we will all do all we can for it. It seems that Jeppe is fated to a life of trouble."
- Lizzie, 28 June 1864 (06-099-P02): "We will call the baby Sarah. I do not think it is a pretty name, but Gale named her that."
- Lizzie, 6 September 1864 (06-109-P04): "They say after she died her eyes poped out of her head. While they were going to bury her, her little brother was taken suddenly, and died [[deleted: illegible]] in two hours he had had a chill or two before, but got up and helped to catch a chicken that morning and ate a hearty breakfast. It is a strange disease. They did not complain of any pain."
- Lizzie Graves, 15 September 1868 (06-041-P01): "About a month ago I saw something that I never witnessed before—three corpses at one sight all to be laid in one family grave yard Cousin [[M. W.?]] and her little infant were laid in one coffin and Mrs. G's little girl in another The coffins were set close together with the lids off so that all three might be seen at a glance while the preacher stood at the head to perform the last religious rite: happy trio!"
- Emma Bell's composition (05-084-P01): "Who can discribe the feelings of them who stand around the death bed of a loved father or mother, brother or sister, and hear from their dying lips the last fond words of love they will ever be permitted to utter."
- L. A. Jetton, 3 March 1878 (06-018-P01): "Dear Cousin you have my deepest sympathy in your great afflictions I loved Cousin Sue on earth I hope to meet her in heaven where Sorrows and partings are unknown"
- L. A. Jetton, 20 March 1883 (06-014-P02): "She exposed herself getting up very early of mornings to look at the Comet which She was very much interested about She took a very Severe Cold and died from the effect of it"
- George's child (05-062-P01, 05-061-P01): "His health became impaired and he realized that his work was done. He died in Ga and / was brought back and buried from the Church that had been his life work."
- George, 3 September 1923 (04-053-P01): "I would love for you to be here, for I get very blue and lonesome at times, and you are all I have that is best to live for. With the number of my years I cant stay here long, so your company is that much more precious. I may survive this trouble and may not. At all events my physical strength will be greatly weakened, and for this reason I wanted the car sold, as my car cranking days are over."
- George, Friday 9 A.M., 1923 (04-061-P03): "It will take a long time to heal the hole in my cheek made by the cancer coming out, but the process will not be painful, only tedious."

### Money trouble, prices, farming

- Jesse, 28 November 1869 (05-130-P04): "Well My [[lost: torn edge]]rop amounted to one Bale [[lost: torn edge]] 434 lbs which paid My debts [[lost: torn edge]] Left me one dollar, the sale and [[lost: torn edge]]ttlement previous to sickness — [[lost: torn edge]] medicine & Dr Bill since Leaves Me about thirty dollars Behind, that I will be able to settle with the sale of Pork this winter"
- Jesse, 30 August 1869 (05-124-P02): "I think that is enough to raise a mans ambition above rotten Corn mark — ((what you say))"
- Henry, 23 June 1860 (05-009-P01): "I have nothing encouraging to write. I have had to pay from 1 to 2 dollars for sheep & have been at a great deal of trouble to find them & expense in collecting my drove."
- Lizzie, 13 November 1864 (06-090-P01): "Father bought a sow and five shoats from her for ten dollars specie, so I reckon we are safe in the meat line for another year. — that is if we can get corn raised to feed them on."
- Lizzie, 13 November 1864 (06-089-P02): "He spoke fully two hours, and a good portion of his sermon was about the depreciation of the currency (I am not talking about the \"old\" and \"new issue\") but the money itself. The \"old issue\" was fully represented to day; and judging from the display have no idea of being repudiated soon."
- Lizzie, undated (06-066-P01): "It is now currently reported and believed, that the wheat which the Farmers carried in before the tythe tax, was due, in order that the Soldiers might get some flour has been sold by speculators. … There was a man here the other day from Nacochitoches who says this is so, and that Magruder is at the head of it."
- Lizzie, 15 April 1864 (06-087-P02): "But I always have and always will think there is something wrong about this cotton trade."
- Lizzie Graves, 10 May 1867 (06-054-P02): "he is going to carry corn off for sale it is selling at $2.00 per bushel)"
- Henry, 6 September 1892 (04-090-P01): "Many are picking & the gin whistles are beginning to sound If we could get fair prices we would be prospering."
- George, undated (04-029-P01): "We will have to hedge on expenses. The Ellis Co. cotton crop is a failure today the eleventh of August. The worms have completely destroyed all the bolls and squares, and blooms are scarcer than I ever saw"
- George, 1923 (04-059-P02): "Good apples here sell from a dollar to a dollar and a half per bushel. Cider fifty cents to a dollar per gallon. Pears 1.00 to 1.50 per bushel."
- George, 30 August 1923 (04-074-P01): "Write it out, dont put down figures for you cant make them with a pencil legible. Those you put down in your letter from Dallas I do not know just what they were, had to guess, and guess work wont do in business."
- Lillard, 22 March 1883 (05-098-P01): "I want you to understand that I am thank God far ahead in this world's goods, and I don't know of a human being to whom I owe a cent, or whom I ever wronged"
- L. A. Jetton, 20 February 1870 (05-121-P01): "Pap is always Complaining about his poverty and That he has a hard way to get along and he is afraid he will come to Starvation Oh you dont know how bad I feel Sometimes but enough of a painful Subject"

### Faith

- Henry, 3 December 1853 (04-095-P01): "It was a beautiful & interesting scene to see children, young & comparatively innocent, pressing into the Kingdom, & thus consecrating their lives to the Redeemer Whilst others, old & gray, could sit unmoved, under the appeals of the Gospel, nor be persuaded to devote the little remainder of their 3 score & ten, to the only Cause worth living for, & the only one that promises aught for the future"
- Henry, 8 September 1851 (04-081-P01): "O that all the Family that have arrived at the years of discretion were gathered into the fold of Christ."
- Lizzie, 6 September 1864 (06-111-P01): "Every one preaches his own doctrine with all his might, consequently discussion [[inserted: is]] rife. … It is only the methodists that are so hostile. the presbyterians are not so afraid of the truth."
- Lizzie, 7 September 1864 (06-112-P01): "In his own language he says \"I tell my brethren that they (the christians) have the bulge on us and we had better let them alone\". This is an odd expression."
- Lizzie, 6 August 1864 (06-116-P01): "We agreed that we did not wish to \"worship the Lord next Sunday in our dirty clothes\" ha ha. You ought to have heard us laughing last night."
- The 1888 endorsement (03-003-P01): "May he realize with the Apostle Paul. \"Woe is me if I preach not the Gospel of Christ\""
- L. Douglas, 16 February 1883 (02-004-P02): "More than eighteen hundred years have been measured by the revolving Earth since Jesus walked the shores and waves of Galilee, but the influence of His words and life, are still felt. And down through the vista of the coming Ages they will be felt till time shall be no more."
- Henry, 6 September 1892 (04-088-P01, 04-089-P01): "Surely California & Sodom & Gomorrah are not unlike / A day in the courts of the Lord is surely worth a thousand in such a place & I surely would make haste to get out, were I there, lest I too might forget God. … I know of nothing so calculated to make us forget as the love of money or the worship of Mammon We have it here."
- Lizzie Graves, 30 September 1867 (06-048-P01): "yet I think I have a greater influence over my brothers now than I ever did and I feel the weight of the responsibility of it Oh! may God assist me to set a right example"
- Sadie, 1893 (05-043-P01): "I told him 'twas against my religious convictions He said \"christianity would never keep you from starving to death besides the majority of christians favored it\" I told him the \"majority\" were on their way to perdition too."
- George's child (05-062-P01): "Though opposing the Sunday School he did not withdraw from the congregation but endeavored to present the truth as he understood it."

### Family affection and humour

- Lizzie, March 20 (06-074-P02): "I know you know how to spell, but you sometimes leave out letters, which causes [[deleted: them]] [[inserted: words]] to be spelt wrong. I know you can soon remedy this, by a little carefulness in writing."
- Lizzie, March 20 (06-074-P03, 06-074-P06): "your letters are sometimes marked \"Due ten cents\". … I expect you will find the same objections to my letter as I did of yours the words stretched to much."
- Lizzie, 19 February 1864 (06-095-P04): "I asked Emmie what to tell uncle Geo. she says tell he do write me little letters. … I never once told you a word about the baby. She is a very pretty, blue eyed fair skinned little May. I wish you could see her."
- Lizzie, 21 April 1865 (06-069-P02): "The thread you sent by J. M. was very acceptable, it will save me many a turn of my \"Piano.\""
- Lizzie, 21 April 1865 (06-070-P01): "take me from friends and Home, and place me among strangers, and I would feel that I was left to pine neglected, and to \"perish in forgetfulness\" — to be poetical."
- Lizzie, 7 September 1864 (06-112-P02): "But I would gladly take supper with you some night, if it consisted of bread and water only."
- Ellen, 12 January 1862 (04-083-P01): "I had plenty of cake and pies at my cabin Christmas day and a big Egg nog. I envited some of the near neighbors to come and dine with me. and about ten oclock I looked out and saw 14 young men and girls riged out comeing I did not know that they were comeing so you see they stormed me."
- Cousin Henry, 29 January 1864 (04-005-P04): "If there is any thing in Reports I have been married to two [[illegible]] in the Country & Started to get a License in the 3d instance. … I was not aware of being so popular - perhaps its the [[illegible]] the bracing Air dont you think -"
- Cousin Henry, 29 January 1864 (04-005-P05): "If so I think it would be a damn reason to raise a Regt & wish you to be my Chf of Staff"
- Lizzie, 28 June 1864 (06-098-P03): "Since the old people have gone to marrying I suppose the young folks will think they have full licence now."
- Lizzie, 16 November 1864 (06-092-P02): "Dr. Mc. [[Curdy?]] is making her a new one; he made a gold nose and then cut a piece of skin out of her forehead and twisted it around and covered the gold. … He says he is going to make her a lip when her nose gets well and then take her for pay."
- L. A. Jetton, 20 February 1870 (05-120-P01): "She tells us that She has had Three [[Divorces?]] but is as lively as a cricket And thinks She would like to marry again if She could get a chance"
- L. A. Jetton (05-121-P01): "Mr Akers Says about his [[Soninlaw?]] he Says The devil owed him a grudge and has paid him off in Soninlaws"
- Sadie, 1893 (05-039-P01): "Elmo remarked if that cow was married, she would have a calf. (with her he meant) Making that a [[test?]] most of the California cows are unmarried."
- Sadie, August (05-048-P01): "Elmo said to me one day \"when a girl marries a young man, she takes his name, doesn't she\", to which I said yes, and he continued how many Headricks will there be then? Bruce was standing by, and put in, I'm going to marry a \"dutch girl\"."
- George to Zella (04-033-P04): "I boiled me two potato's and six eggs yesterday evening, and Ruth gave me a glass of plum jelly and I have plenty of bread, and I reboiled the Molasses and strained it through the new sifter. So I am prepared to get fat."
- George to Zella (04-038-P05): "yesterday I put up a gallon and a quart of preserves all by myself. I had 12 pounds of peaches, and 9 pounds sugar, began at sun up and got through by noon. I did not use a single drop of water."
- George to Zella (04-030-P02): "Came up today and had a real civilized dinner with [[Bros?]] Duncan. I dont see why all women can't cook. Its so much nicer —"
- George to Zella (04-034-P04): "So my prospects for baching grows brighter from day to day. Sure I live better, and my next cook must look refined."
- George, 3 September 1923 (04-048-P03): "Your very brown letter just to hand. With the aid of a magnifying glass I managed to read it mostly. Please use ink, or rough paper that a pencil will make a mark on."
- Maybel/Marybee Jetton, 8 April 1874 (06-021-P02, 06-022-P01): "I have Started me a Scrapbook tell Cousin George to [[deleted: send me]] Send me some pretty verses to / put in it. Dear cousin you dont know how much I should like to see my old home and friends once more."

### Weather and seasons

- Lizzie, 22 April 1865 (06-070-P02): "Today has been bright and clear, quite an uncommon thing: the wind has been from the north since day before Yesterday, and last night we had frost enough to kill most of our beans, though I do not think the fruit is injured."
- Lizzie, 13 November 1864 (06-090-P02): "The wind was blowing cold from the north on last monday evening we were all sitting round the fire, when we heard [[coo a night owl?]] we looked out and sure enough there she was."
- Lizzie (06-081-P01): "It is a horrid blowing wind from the south, every thing is just getting green enough to look pretty."
- Henry, 8 September 1851 (04-080-P01): "It has not rained here for so long that I forget when it did rain last, every thing is very much parched up."
- Jesse, 30 August 1869 (05-123-P02): "The weather is extremely hot and dry in this section since the ((flood)) as I term it and in consequence a great deal of sickness below this Mostly Chills and fever"
- George, 30 August 1923 (04-077-P01): "Plenty of rain here. Corn is green and green mostly in good roasting ear. Cotton is said to be good, also the Boll Weevil. Days are warm; 80 to 90, nights cool, sleep under cover every night."

### The Tennessee inheritance

- Lillard, 30 December 1882 (05-096-P04): "Whatever the law gives you is right and if Mrs Alexander had not wanted the land to go to her kin she could have willed it otherwise So she left the law of the land as her will,"
- Lewis Jetton, 12 February 1883 (05-112-P01, 05-112-P03): "Mr Lilard is working for big fees – of course you ought to kno what is best [[deleted: illegible]] for you to do in the case … I would not give power to no atorny till I was acquainted with him / L Jetton / The above you must keep in secrecy in the family"
- Lillard, 22 March 1883 (05-099-P01): "It looks like a blind man ought to see through this"
- Lillard, 22 March 1883 (05-102-P01): "I do use two cups of Coffee per day, I smoke tobacco and sometimes a little Mullein prescribed by a Methodist Preacher several years ago, but my respect for ladies is too great to allow me to chew tobacco, and this is the extent of my stimulants"
- L. A. Jetton, 20 March 1883 (06-015-P01): "he told them Cousin Rebecca will was stolen out of her draw two weeks before her death he is willing to give up the land does not wish to have a law Suit but wants to buy the land of the heirs"

---

## E. Conflicts and corrections

Where the papers contradict, qualify or fail to support the facts listed in the questions, or contradict themselves.

1. **Sylvana endorsement date.** The finding aid says 1883 (01-006-P01) and the slip "Sept 1888" (03-002-P01); the manuscript reads "Sept 1888" (03-005-P01). The papers support 1888.
2. **George's death place.** The memoir says "He died in Ga" (05-062-P01); the 1923 envelopes are read "UNION CITY … CA." (04-055-P02, 04-064-P02) and once "GA" (04-071-P02); the letter rules out returning "by Nashville … over the Cumberland Mountains" (04-052-P03), a route from Georgia, not California. The papers support Georgia; the "CA." readings need image check.
3. **"Rusk" versus "Hood" County, 1875.** This edition reads Jeppe Douglas's acknowledgment "County of [[Rusk?]]" (04-043-P06); the family edition reads Hood County at Glen Rose. Jesse's own 1869 land was in Hood County (05-132-P01). Unresolved; Hood is better supported.
4. **Henry Douglas's initials.** He signs "J. H. Douglas" on the 1875 quitclaim (04-042-P03) but "Henry Douglas", "H. Douglas", "H Douglas" elsewhere; a slip calls the doctor "Dr. (L. Douglas)" (06-052-P01). The papers do not settle his full name.
5. **Henry's December 1853 letter.** Slip "Dec. 30, 1853" (04-092-P01); manuscript "Dec. 3d 1853" (04-093-P01). The letter mentions meeting David Gaslin "last monday evening" and a meeting that "closed last monday night"; either date is possible from the text. The manuscript reading is 3 December.
6. **George's sister who died in early 1865.** Henry's condolence of 13 March 1865 on "the Death of your Sister" (04-010-P04) does not fit the question's Emily (d. 1850) or Mary (d. 1861). It may be Rebecca Douglas Winniford (d. 1865 per the question), whose heirs held land by 1875 (05-158-P03); the papers do not name the sister.
7. **Two letters numbered 16.** Lizzie's letters of 19–21 August 1864 and 6–7 September 1864 are both "No 16" (06-101-P01, 06-109-P01); the June letter is "No. 13" but the slip says 18 (06-096-P01, 06-097-P01).
8. **Earl's middle name.** Manuscript "Earl Stewart Douglas" (05-124-P02); slip "Earl Stuart Douglas" (05-122-P01); the mother-in-law is "the Old Stewarts" (05-131-P02).
9. **L. A. Jetton's given name.** Slips read "(Lewis?)" (05-118-P01, 06-023-P01) but the Readyville letters to Lizzie and Sue are written by a woman ("the children", "my Soninlaw", 06-018-P01) who signs "L A Jetton", while the Murfreesboro letter of 12 February 1883 to "My Sisters" is signed "L Jetton" (05-112-P03) and the envelope "From Lewis Jetton" (05-105-P01). Two different people may share the initials (inference); the slips' identification is not secure.
10. **Maybel/Marybee.** Slip "Maybel ?" (06-019-P01); signature read "[[Maybey?]] Jetton" (06-022-P01); "Marybee" (06-025-P01); "Mary Lees Babe" (06-013-P01); "may beth" (06-018-P01).
11. **Winniford spellings.** "Winneford" (finding aid), "Winniford", "Winiford", "Rebeca Winniford" occur (01-005-P01, 05-145-P02, 05-141-P04, 06-003-P01).
12. **Inventory date.** Slip "Oct 3, 1874" (05-150-P01); oath "October 5th AD 1874" (05-151-P03).
13. **Deed dates versus recording.** The 1886 deed was recorded in 1896 (05-155-P03); the 1891 printed deed was recorded 1894 (05-139-P03); the Winniford heirs' second deed of 22 July 1891 was recorded 1896 (06-011-P04). The slips give filing dates as if they were deed dates (05-152-P01, 06-007-P01).
14. **Sadie's 1893 letter.** Slip "[[Sat?]] 29, 1893" (05-035-P01); letter "Sep, 29th, 1893" (05-036-P01); envelope postmark "AUG 9 [[1894?]]" (05-035-P03).
15. **The 1921 envelope.** Addressed to "Mrs. Sara Headrick, Red Oak" (05-049-P01), it accompanies an incomplete letter whose contents match the Bardsdale letters of the 1890s; the pairing may be accidental.
16. **The February 1st note.** This edition reads "Feb 1st 1865" and a Laura attribution from the slip "Feb. 11, 1865(?)" (06-082-P01, 06-083-P01); the family edition reads 1863 and a signature "Hat". Unresolved.
17. **Uncle Alfred's dates.** "he died on the 5th of June from a wound Recd on the 18th of May" (04-002-P04) and "He died late Sunday evening the 5th of this month, just 18 days after he was wounded" (06-097-P02) agree; 5 June 1864 was a Sunday.
18. **The Claunch murder.** Henry names "a Mrs Claunch" (04-017-P03); the sisters' fragment reads "Mrs [[Stinnet?]]" (06-063-P01) for what is evidently the same September 1863 case; the surname reading in the fragment is doubtful.
19. **Warren's unit.** No unit appears in the papers; the 21st Texas Infantry and the rank of 2nd lieutenant are not stated anywhere (see C.5).
20. **George's arrival at Sabine Pass, 1863.** Not established by the supplied papers (see C.3).
21. **The 1854 "loan".** No 1854 loan exists in the scans; the 1852–54 papers are Sublett/Bass agency documents (C.7).
22. **The Sunday School.** The memoir says George opposed it at Sylvana (05-062-P01); the Readyville Jettons and Lizzie Graves in Alabama taught Sunday School (06-021-P02, 06-042-P01). A difference of practice within the wider family, not a contradiction of fact.

---

## F. Ten most important or interesting findings

1. **The papers fix George Douglas's death: 23 September 1923, away from home in Georgia under cancer treatment, and his burial from the Sylvana church.** "My father continued his labors with the Church at Sylvana until his death Sept 23 1923 … He died in Ga and / was brought back and buried from the Church that had been his life work" (05-062-P01, 05-061-P01), corroborated by the slip (05-060-P01) and by the run of "Papa" letters from Union City of 30 August–12 September 1923 describing radium treatment of a cancer of the cheek (04-048 to 04-077). This gives the book its last chapter in George's own voice.
2. **George's Civil War station was Sabine Pass in 1864, in a company C of a battalion later a regiment, furloughed by lot.** Address "George Douglas / Co C / [[illegible]] Regt / Sab Pass" (04-003-P01); "your Batt." (06-094-P03); "you have been [[illegible]] in the Reg." (06-114-P02); furlough "number 13" and "Now that Walker is in command" (06-104-P02); Evans "left Sabine on the 31st" (06-110-P02). The Sabine Pass journal itself is not in the supplied scans, so the September 1863 question stays open.
3. **Warren Douglas is in the same Sabine command, and "Griffin" had "a particular spite" at him.** "we have not heard from Warren but look for him and Beck down. Griffin or somebody else must have a particular spite at Warren" and "he would be here on the 10th of Dec. on his way back to Sabine" (06-091-P02, 06-092-P01); with "Kind Regards to Warren & John" in a letter to George in camp (04-011-P04). Together with Warren's residence in Johnson County in 1887 (05-154-P04), this is the papers' whole contribution to Warren's war and gives a concrete lead (Griffin's command at Sabine Pass) for the doubted 21st Texas Infantry claim.
4. **The family was Stone-Campbell ("Christian Church") by 1851, and the home congregation was Liberty at Red Oak before it was Sylvana.** Henry from Palestine on Susannah's conversion, "gathered into the fold of Christ" (04-081-P01); the 1853 co-operation meeting (04-094-P01); Lizzie's 1864 account of "the christians" versus the Methodists at Liberty (06-111-P01, 06-112-P01); "the Church of Christ worshiping at Sylvana" in 1888 (03-003-P01). The book can date the affiliation a generation earlier than the 1888 endorsement.
5. **N. L. Douglas was the Texas land agent and attorney-in-fact of Rutherford County, Tennessee, landowners in 1849–54, and the family's 640 acres lay in the same A. J. Porter survey on Sublett's Creek that he redeemed for Sublett in 1851.** "my friend N L Douglas of Ellis County" (05-025-P01); "N L Douglas as agent for G A Sublett … 1280 acres of Land lying on Subletts Creek … listed to A J Porter" (05-021-P01); "640 acres of land a part of the A. J. Porter 1280 acre Survey located on Subletts creek" (05-151-P02). This is the earliest documented Texas activity (a tax sale of 4 February 1850) and explains how the family came to the Porter survey.
6. **The 1874–75 estate papers give the family's exact economic position and how the land passed.** Inventory: 94 acres in cultivation at $15, 546 unimproved at $5, 9 horses, 20 cattle, 14 hogs, 20 bee hives, tools (05-151-P02); Mary deeded each child a portion and the heirs quitclaimed (04-042-P02); Mary gave Lizzie 82 acres for life care (05-158-P02); in 1886–91 all the heirs conveyed Mary's remaining thirty acres to Lizzie "for and in consideration of Services rendered to the Said Mary Douglas during her last years of life" (05-136-P02). Lizzie, the unmarried daughter and the family's letter-keeper, became the landholder, and George bought and sold within the same survey (06-003-P01).
7. **Jesse (Jeppe) Douglas lost his wife Gail in childbirth on 23 April 1864 and the baby, Sarah, was raised at Brushy by his sisters; that baby became Sadie Headrick of Bardsdale, California, mother of Bruce Headrick who financed the Red Oak land in 1939.** "Jeppe is again a widower. Gail died last Saturday (23d)" (06-106-P02); "We will call the baby Sarah … Gale named her that" (06-099-P02); "To Daughter Sarah (Sal) from Jesse Douglas" (05-126-P01); "Sarah Gail (Douglas) Headrick" (05-035-P01) with sons Elmo and Bruce (05-037-P01); "Loan of Bruce Headrick … Payable to Assn. and Bruce Headrick and Zella Douglas" (04-040-P07). One thread runs from 1864 to 1939.
8. **Mary Douglas's mother was a Wright of Rutherford County and Mary was a first cousin of Rebecca T. (Wright) Alexander, whose 1882 intestacy set off the 1883 lawsuit correspondence.** "your Cousin Mrs Alexander daughter of your uncle Jacob Wright" (05-095-P01); "daughters of Rebecca Goodloe" (05-086-P01); the Dill, Brasher, Thompson Wright, Jetton and Goodloe cousinage (05-095-P01, 05-092-P01). This is the papers' richest genealogical seam for the Goodloe side and it names Murfreesboro court records to pursue.
9. **Uncle Alfred died of a wound on Sunday 5 June 1864, eighteen days after being hit on 18 May, and Aunt Bettie collapsed at the news and left for Arkansas that November.** (04-002-P04, 06-097-P02, 06-098-P01, 06-089-P02, 06-092-P04). Two independent letters agree on the dates; the identity of Alfred (a Douglas? an Orr? a Campbell?) is not stated and is a target for the next search.
10. **Lizzie Douglas's letters are the book's narrative core: the Claunch murder, the tithe-tax speculators and "old Magruder", the hanging of the Stiles boys and "this mob law", the militia armed with sticks, the gold nose, and the sisters' laughter.** (06-063-P01, 06-066-P01, 06-070-P02, 06-071-P01, 06-095-P03, 06-092-P02, 06-116-P01). They also show the household of 1864: Father and Mother, Lizzie, Laura, Sue and her children, Joda, the baby Sarah, "Ruth, sisters black girl whom we have hired" (06-104-P02), pet deer and turkeys on Brushy Creek.

---

## G. Suggested next records

- **ACU, George Douglas Papers, folders 4–7** (not supplied): the day book from 4 January 1830 (Folder 5), the Civil War journal on Sabine Pass and the governor's land certificate (scope note), the photographs, George's notebook and poems (Folder 7), the 1863 letters to George and Warren and the Winniford and Bell letters (Folder 6), Emily Douglas's songs and Rebecca Goodloe's letters (Folder 4). These answer the day-book, journal and certificate questions directly (01-003-P09, 01-006-P02 to 01-006-P05).
- **Ellis County Deed Records, Waxahachie:** Deed Record A p. 163 (Sublett power of attorney, 1852, 05-026-P02); Deed Record O pp. 2–3 and 3/344 (the 1875 quitclaim and Mary's deed to Lizzie, 04-045-P03, 05-157-P03); Vol. 47 pp. 228–9 (George to Lizzie, 1887, 06-006-P02); Book 62 p. 590 (Winniford heirs, 1891, 05-144-P03); Book 46 p. 358 (1894, 05-139-P03); Book 92 pp. 574 and 576 (1896, 06-011-P04, 05-155-P03); Vol. Z p. 58 (the Geo. Douglas 80-acre tract) and Vol. 179 p. 362 (J. H. Hedrick 20 acres) (04-046-P03). Also the deeds by which Mary Douglas conveyed portions to each child before February 1875 and the deed from Mary to the heirs of Rebecca Winniford (04-042-P02, 05-145-P02), the 1917 cemetery deed, and any deed to a "Church lot" adjoining the Winniford tract (06-004-P01).
- **Ellis County Probate, 1874–75:** the estate of N. L. Douglas (inventory sworn 5 October 1874 before Wm Hancock J.P., appraisers E. A. Norman and W R Duff, 05-151-P03) for a death date, letters of administration and the family's slaveholding or not before 1865 through any earlier probate or tax roll.
- **Ellis County tax rolls, 1850s–60s** (Texas State Library, county tax rolls on microfilm): whether N. L. or George Douglas was assessed for enslaved persons, and whether either held the office of assessor-collector (the papers show only Navarro County's W. B. McCabe, 05-021-P01).
- **Navarro County Deed Records, Corsicana:** Book A pp. 433–34 (Crow to Sublett, 1849) and Book C p. 181 (1851 redemption) (05-031-P02, 05-034-P02), and the assessor's sale records of 4 February 1850 and 25 July 1850 (05-021-P01, 05-033-P02).
- **Texas General Land Office, Austin:** A. J. Porter 1,280-acre survey, Ellis County, patented 18 June 1849 (05-153-P02), Abstract 829 (04-041-P06); Jasper County second-class certificate No 246 of 10 January 1840 to Robert Crow (05-029-P01); the G. C. Briscoe headright in Hood County (05-132-P03); and George Douglas's own certificate from the governor once its number is read from the ACU original.
- **NARA compiled service records (Confederate, Texas):** George Douglas and Warren Douglas, searching the Sabine Pass garrison units of 1864 (the papers give "Co C", "Batt.", "Reg.", "Griffin", "Walker in command", furlough by lot; 04-003-P01, 06-091-P02, 06-104-P02); Henry (cousin) in "Co G Browns Regt" (04-005-P06); Dock in "Co. G. Bates' Reg" (06-091-P02); James and Lafayette (Orr) in a Texas brigade in the Army of Tennessee, "old Co I", wounded 22 July 1864 (04-021-P03, 04-023-P04); Uncle Alfred, wounded 18 May 1864, died 5 June 1864 (06-097-P02). Also Texas Confederate pension files for George, Warren and Henry.
- **Rutherford County, Tennessee, Chancery and Circuit Court records, Murfreesboro:** the partition suits over the estate of Rebecca T. Alexander (died 11 November 1882), Lillard's bill against A. M. Alexander and D. Travis, Chancellor Burton's death in term, the 1883 decree and Supreme Court appeal (05-087, 05-107-P01). The bill would name every Goodloe, Dill, Brasher, Thompson Wright and Jetton heir and their relationships, i.e. Mary Douglas's whole maternal family. Also Rutherford County deeds and marriages for the Goodloe–Wright marriage and for Nathaniel Douglas's Tennessee years (the 1829 due bill and 1832 note, 05-022, 05-012).
- **Johnson County, Texas:** District Clerk Jno. T. Odom's acknowledgment of Warren Douglas, 27 January 1887 (05-154-P04), and Johnson County deeds and marriages (the Evans marriage; the widow Smith of Johnson Co., 06-098-P03).
- **Hood, Erath and Travis Counties:** Jesse Douglas's Kickapoo Creek land (Hood, 1869, 05-132-P01); Cross Creek/Dripping Springs, Travis County, 1869 (05-123-P01, 05-133-P05); Alexander and Carlton, Erath County, 1884 (05-127-P01); the Erath acknowledgment of Nancy and Earl Douglas, 4 June 1891 (05-142-P02), and Jesse's probate.
- **Anderson County (Palestine):** the Christian Church built after 1851 (04-080-P01); Dr Kendrick's practice and Henry's partnership (05-003-P01).
- **Church records:** Liberty (Red Oak) and Sylvana congregations, Ellis County; the "Advocate" and "the A.W." (probably Stone-Campbell periodicals, inference) for George's camp-meeting notices and Duckworth's circular (05-040-P01, 04-077-P01); Rawlins cemetery (04-037-P01); the Douglas Cemetery deed of 1917.
- **Federal Land Bank of Houston / Ellis National Farm Loan Association records:** loans 92466J and 336394J to Bruce Headrick, application 189553, 1939 (04-040, 04-041).
- **Union City, Georgia, and Bardsdale/Santa Paula, Ventura County, California:** the "Dr Bird" cancer establishment where George was treated in 1923 (04-054-P02) and the Headrick store and Chadsey neighbours of the 1890s (05-037-P01, 05-047-P02); Ventura County notary James McKee's record of the Robisons' 1893 acknowledgment (05-137-P04).
- **Readyville and Woodbury, Cannon County, Tennessee:** the Jetton family (L. A., Albert, Lewis, Marybee, Adele, Willie, Charlie) and New Hope Church (06-021-P02, 06-024-P01); Woodbury for "Mollie" (05-057-P02).
